IT Strategy 
12 
Q. 
What is the Company’s general IT strategy?  
13 
A. 
As explained in our initial testimony, the Company’s IT 
14 
strategy focuses on five areas and guides the work we do. 
15 
These areas are Security, Operational Excellence, Customer 
16 
Experience, Technology Modernization, and Innovation. 
17 
Q. 
Where does cybersecurity fit into IT? 
18 
A. 
Cybersecurity is embedded into IT investment decisions, 
19 
operational planning, and service delivery. It is a 
20 
critical underpinning of Con Edison's IT strategy, 
21 
protecting systems, networks, and data from threats and 
22

CASES 25-E-0072, 25-G-0073 
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK, INC. 
INFORMATION TECHNOLOGY PANEL -- REBUTTAL 
 
5 
 
unauthorized access. As digital infrastructure becomes more 
1 
integral, cybersecurity provides the integrity, 
2 
confidentiality, and availability of information assets. It 
3 
encompasses practices, tools, and policies to safeguard 
4 
data, mitigate risks, and prevent breaches through 
5 
controls, monitoring, and reducing vulnerabilities. By 
6 
embedding cybersecurity into IT frameworks, Con Edison 
7 
enhances resilience against threats, complies with 
8 
regulations, and maintains customer and stakeholder trust, 
9 
thereby forming a foundation for secure and reliable 
10 
operations. Specific cyber projects are covered in the 
11 
Cyber and Physical Security Panel, ensuring all IT 
12 
components meet security requirements.  
13

14

16

17 
design, operate and manage IT infrastructure, applications 
18 
and services. The Company used the term “byproduct” in its 
19 
initial testimony merely to note that the Cyber and Physical 
20 
Security Panel would describe projects and programs where

IT.

Key Components of IT Infrastructure 
4 
Q. 
What is IT infrastructure and what are its key components? 
5 
A.  IT infrastructure consists of hardware, software, 
6 
networking components, and services to manage and deliver 
7 
IT services. IT infrastructure serves as the foundation to 
8 
run and operate applications, and includes servers, storage 
9 
systems, mainframe systems, network devices, operating 
10 
systems, and applications that facilitate business 
11 
operations and communications. It provides application and 
12 
communication reliability, scalability, and security.  
13 
Q. 
Please describe what is meant by hardware. 
14 
A. 
Hardware refers to the physical components of a computer or 
15 
IT system, including devices such as central processing 
16 
unit(s) (CPU), memory, servers, storage, mainframe, 
17 
laptops, desktop tablets, and peripherals, such as 
18 
keyboards and monitors. Hardware enables computing tasks. 
19 
Q. 
Please explain infrastructure software.  
20 
A.  Infrastructure software is comprised of: 
21 


--- Page 8 ---
CASES 25-E-0072, 25-G-0073 
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK, INC. 
INFORMATION TECHNOLOGY PANEL -- REBUTTAL 
 
7 
 
• Operating systems on laptops and desktops; 
1 
• Server and storage management systems; 
2 
• Server virtualization tools;  
3 
• Backup and recovery technology; 
4 
• Mainframe management systems; 
5 
• Network operations tools; and 
6 
• Cybersecurity tools to protect devices and networks.  
7 
Q. 
Does the Company have a mainframe system? 
8 
A. 
Yes. However, as networking and cloud computing have grown 
9 
over the years, the Company has moved most of its systems 
10 
from the mainframe - as mainframes do not have the 
11 
computing power that networks and clouds do. It is also 
12 
becoming more difficult to hire programmers that know 
13 
mainframe languages.   
14 
Q. 
What is the Company’s plan for its mainframe? 
15 
A.  The Company plans to decommission the mainframe by December 
16 
2028, after the remaining mainframe applications and data 
17 
are fully migrated to new platforms.   
18 
Q. 
What is networking? 
19 
A. 
Networking connects computers and devices to share 
20 
resources, including data, and communicate with one 
21 
another. The Company uses various networking technologies, 
22 
including fiber optics networks, Local Area Networks (LAN), 
23 


--- Page 9 ---
CASES 25-E-0072, 25-G-0073 
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK, INC. 
INFORMATION TECHNOLOGY PANEL -- REBUTTAL 
 
8 
 
Wide Area Networks (WAN), and Voice Over IP (VOIP) to 
1 
support operations. This technology allows for information 
2 
exchange within local networks or over the internet, 
3 
including emails and file transfers.   
4 
Q. 
What is an on-premises data center and does the Company 
5 
have on-premises data centers? 
6 
A. 
On-premises (or on prem) refers to IT infrastructure and 
7 
software that are hosted and managed within an 
8 
organization’s physical data center, rather than being 
9 
accessed through the cloud. This setup requires the 
10 
organization to maintain its own infrastructure. The 
11 
Company has four on-premises data centers. 
12 
Q. 
Are data centers part of IT infrastructure? 
13 
A. 
Yes. Data centers are a critical part of IT infrastructure, 
14 
housing applications, servers, storage systems, and 
15 
networking equipment.  
16 
Q. 
What is meant by cloud computing? 
17 
A. 
The cloud is a network of remote servers that store, 
18 
manage, and process data over the internet. The cloud 
19 
allows users to access resources and services on-demand. 
20 
The cloud enables scalable computing power and storage 
21 
without the need for an on-premises data center 
22 


--- Page 10 ---
CASES 25-E-0072, 25-G-0073 
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK, INC. 
INFORMATION TECHNOLOGY PANEL -- REBUTTAL 
 
9 
 
infrastructure and networking, thereby, facilitating 
1 
flexibility and cost-efficiency. Cloud services are 
2 
provided by major technology vendors, such as Microsoft, 
3 
Amazon Web Services and Google. Cloud providers operate and 
4 
maintain these platforms and data centers, including 
5 
performing upgrades and patching, without requiring any 
6 
user actions. 
7 
Q. 
What is the Company’s data center and cloud strategy? 
8 
A. 
The Company’s strategic approach is to migrate and 
9 
consolidate workloads within existing data center 
10 
footprints, while also transitioning workloads and 
11 
modernizing applications. The goal is to maximize resource 
12 
utilization within the current infrastructure and update 
13 
and modernize applications to be able to use the cloud. As 
14 
an example, the Company migrated our EBS application to 
15 
cloud servers, enabling the Company to free up our on-
16 
premises data center footprint for new workloads. Balancing 
17 
workload and migrating from the data center footprint is 
18 
essential to reducing the need to build a new data center, 
19 
at an approximate cost of $50 million.   
20 
Q. 
How do IT infrastructure components support business 
21 
applications? 
22

10 
 
A. 
IT infrastructure components provide the necessary 
1 
resources and connectivity that enable business 
2 
applications to operate efficiently and reliably. 
3 
Key Components of Business Applications 
4 
Q. 
What are business applications? 
5 
A. 
Business applications are software programs designed to 
6 
perform specific tasks or functions for users to manage 
7 
work, both administratively and operationally.  They can 
8 
range from a single department application to complex, 
9 
multi-department applications. Examples include a Law 
10 
Department Work Management platform that may have ten 
11 
integrations to other applications for Law to manage its 
12 
day-to-day work, to our Finance platform that has very 
13 
complex processes and hundreds of integrations and controls 
14 
for the accurate creation of the Company’s financial 
15 
statements. 
16 
Q. 
Does the Company have an overall IT strategy for 
17 
applications? 
18 
A. 
Yes. Our IT strategy consists of two main approaches: (1) 
19 
we purchase and implement enterprise-wide solutions that 
20 
enable our business units to efficiently manage day-to-day 
21 
operations, and (2) when suitable solutions are not 
22 


--- Page 12 ---
CASES 25-E-0072, 25-G-0073 
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK, INC. 
INFORMATION TECHNOLOGY PANEL -- REBUTTAL 
 
11 
 
available in the industry, we develop applications 
1 
internally to address specific needs within our business 
2 
units. 
3 
Q. 
What is meant by an enterprise-wide solution? 
4 
A. 
An enterprise-wide solution refers to software or platforms 
5 
provided by large vendors, such as Microsoft (M365) or 
6 
Oracle (which include applications like HR, Payroll, 
7 
Finance, Supply Chain, and Billing), that are implemented 
8 
across the entire Company.  As noted in our initial 
9 
testimony, we are standardizing and transitioning to these 
10 
larger platforms. For example, enterprise standards include 
11 
Oracle’s Human Capital Management, IBM’s Maximo Work and 
12 
Asset Management and Esri GIS platforms.  These platforms 
13 
are used by many employees to do their daily work. 
14 
Q. 
How do business applications fit into the overall IT 
15 
process? 
16 
A. 
Business applications integrate with IT infrastructure and 
17 
other systems for smooth data flow and operational 
18 
efficiency. These applications collect and analyze data, 
19 
providing interfaces and functionality for end users that 
20 
inform decision-making and strategic planning. Throughout 
21 
their lifecycle, they are managed by IT teams for security, 
22 


--- Page 13 ---
CASES 25-E-0072, 25-G-0073 
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK, INC. 
INFORMATION TECHNOLOGY PANEL -- REBUTTAL 
 
12 
 
compliance, and alignment with business goals. Business 
1 
applications such as Oracle EBS Finance and Supply Chain, 
2 
Oracle Human Capital Management (HCM), Oracle Customer Care 
3 
and Billing (CC&B), Oracle’s Outage Management System 
4 
(OMS), and IBM’s Maximo Work & Asset Management platforms 
5 
are examples of enterprise platforms that are key parts of 
6 
the IT process. 
7 
Infrastructure and Application Maintenance 
8 
Q. 
Must IT applications and infrastructure be upgraded and/or 
9 
patched? 
10 
A. 
Yes. Applications and infrastructure require regular 
11 
maintenance, including upgrades and patches, to maintain 
12 
optimal performance, security, and compliance. These 
13 
updates enhance functionality, fix vulnerabilities and 
14 
defects, maintain system reliability and compatibility with 
15 
evolving technology and infrastructure, and reduce the risk 
16 
of data breaches. Staff’s testimony suggests that upgrades 
17 
allow for a la carte choosing of features that will be 
18 
included in a given upgrade, which is generally not the 
19 
case. 
20 
Q. 
What is an update? 
21 


--- Page 14 ---
CASES 25-E-0072, 25-G-0073 
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK, INC. 
INFORMATION TECHNOLOGY PANEL -- REBUTTAL 
 
13 
 
A. 
An update is the process of enhancing software or hardware 
1 
according to the vendor’s roadmap, typically including 
2 
improved security, performance enhancements, and new 
3 
features. Regular updates are essential for maintaining 
4 
vendor support, staying current with supported technologies 
5 
(operating systems, databases, programming languages, 
6 
etc.), and protecting against potential cybersecurity 
7 
threats. 
8 
Q. 
What is a patch? 
9 
A. 
A patch is a software update designed to address specific 
10 
issues, vulnerabilities, or bugs in applications, operating 
11 
systems, databases, or hardware (such as routers and 
12 
firewalls). Vendors regularly release patches and applying 
13 
them is critical for maintaining system security and 
14 
stability, as they help protect against cyber threats and 
15 
can also enhance overall performance. 
16 
Lifecycle Management of IT Products and Platforms 
17 
Q. 
How do IT product lifecycles relate to business 
18 
applications? 
19 
A. 
IT product lifecycles are closely related to business 
20 
applications because they effectively manage the technology 
21 
supporting these applications throughout its lifespan. This 
22 


--- Page 15 ---
CASES 25-E-0072, 25-G-0073 
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK, INC. 
INFORMATION TECHNOLOGY PANEL -- REBUTTAL 
 
14 
 
lifecycle management encompasses planning, development, 
1 
deployment, maintenance, and retirement of IT products and 
2 
platforms. A well-managed IT lifecycle provides the stable 
3 
and reliable infrastructure that business applications 
4 
depend on to operate smoothly. By keeping IT products 
5 
updated and secure, lifecycle management aligns business 
6 
applications with organizational goals, ultimately 
7 
improving processes.  
8 
Q. 
What is the IT infrastructure and networking lifecycle? 
9 
A. 
Similar to application product lifecycles, this lifecycle 
10 
includes planning, development or configuration, deployment 
11 
maintenance, optimization, and retirement.  
12 
Q. 
What is the lifecycle of IT products and platforms?  
13 
A. 
The lifecycle of IT products and platforms varies based on 
14 
factors, like vendor support, technology, business 
15 
requirements, and the pace of technological advancements. 
16 
Most vendors typically support their products and platforms 
17 
for two to five years, during which they release new 
18 
versions. Organizations regularly assess their IT products 
19 
and platforms to confirm they remain effective, reliable, 
20 
secure, and aligned with strategic objectives. 
21 
Q. 
What is a vendor roadmap? 
22 


--- Page 16 ---
CASES 25-E-0072, 25-G-0073 
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK, INC. 
INFORMATION TECHNOLOGY PANEL -- REBUTTAL 
 
15 
 
A. 
A vendor roadmap is a strategic plan outlining a vendor's 
1 
planned future product developments, features, and 
2 
enhancements over a specified timeline and it provides 
3 
dates as to when their platform will not be supported. The 
4 
roadmap provides insights into the vendor's priorities, 
5 
goals, and direction, and helps customers understand how 
6 
the vendor intends to evolve its offerings to align with 
7 
market trends. IT and business units leverage these 
8 
roadmaps to properly plan for either upgrades or migration 
9 
to new platforms and incorporate those costs into our long 
10 
term plan.  
11 
Q. 
Do these products and platforms become obsolete over time? 
12 
A. 
Yes. Given the pace of computing evolution, products become 
13 
obsolete within two to five years.  As products and 
14 
platforms become obsolete, operational issues and 
15 
vulnerabilities associated with the product become more 
16 
frequent. As new technologies emerge, older systems may no 
17 
longer meet current demands or integrate effectively.  
18 
Regular updates and assessments are essential to mitigate 
19 
obsolescence risk. 
20 
Software Development Lifecycle 
21 
Q. 
Please explain the software development lifecycle. 
22 


--- Page 17 ---
CASES 25-E-0072, 25-G-0073 
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK, INC. 
INFORMATION TECHNOLOGY PANEL -- REBUTTAL 
 
16 
 
A. 
As discussed above, the Software Development Lifecycle 
1 
(SDLC) consists of several key phases: design, build, test, 
2 
implementation stabilization, and maintenance and support.  
3 
Q. 
Please explain each phase of the SDLC at a high level. 
4 
A. 
The phases are: 
5 
• The design phase identifies, defines, and documents IT 
6 
project requirements. 
7 
• The build phase involves constructing and deploying the 
8 
planned solution through software installation, hardware 
9 
configuration, and data migration. 
10 
• The testing phase validates the solution through unit, 
11 
integration, and user acceptance testing to address any 
12 
issues.  
13 
• Implementation focuses on user training and support for a 
14 
smooth transition to the new system. 
15 
• Stabilization occurs immediately after implementation, 
16 
when a new system or application has moved into the live 
17 
production environment and generally lasts three months. 
18 
• Maintenance and support refer to the ongoing activities 
19 
that occur once a system or application has been 
20 
implemented and stabilized. 
21 


--- Page 18 ---
CASES 25-E-0072, 25-G-0073 
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK, INC. 
INFORMATION TECHNOLOGY PANEL -- REBUTTAL 
 
17 
 
Q. 
Are there different methods of implementing software 
1 
products and system? 
2 
A. 
Generally, there are two methods of implementing software 
3 
products and systems – Waterfall and Agile, both of which 
4 
fall within SDLC. 
5 
Q. 
Please explain Waterfall and Agile methodologies. 
6 
A. 
Waterfall and Agile approach process differently: 
7 
• Waterfall framework is a traditional project 
8 
management approach that follows a linear and sequential 
9 
process. A plan is developed and agreed to by all parties 
10 
to the project. Each phase must be completed before 
11 
moving on to the next. Waterfall adheres strictly to the 
12 
initial scope and requirements, making it less flexible 
13 
in adapting to changes during the project lifecycle.  
14 
• The Agile framework looks at the overall process but 
15 
takes changes one step at a time, allowing flexibility 
16 
for iterative changes.  It emphasizes flexibility, 
17 
collaboration, and continuous improvements. 
18 
Q. 
What project management framework does the Company use to 
19 
implement an IT project?  
20 
A. 
The Company uses both frameworks, depending on the project 
21 
or system.   
22 
Q. 
When does the Company use Waterfall framework?  
23 


--- Page 19 ---
CASES 25-E-0072, 25-G-0073 
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK, INC. 
INFORMATION TECHNOLOGY PANEL -- REBUTTAL 
 
18 
 
A. 
Our large enterprise platforms, such as HR, Payroll, 
1 
Billing, GIS, Finance, and Supply Chain, initially follow 
2 
the Waterfall approach. Once the platform(s) is built and 
3 
operational using Waterfall, we move to the Agile 
4 
methodology to enhance and evolve the system through 
5 
iterative improvements and ongoing collaboration with 
6 
stakeholders. 
7 
Q. 
Does the Company agree that using an Agile framework is a 
8 
relevant justification for reducing project scope to fit 
9 
within Staff’s recommended budget? 
10 
A. 
No. While the Agile framework allows for quicker re-
11 
prioritization and easier scaling than a Waterfall-managed 
12 
project, using Agile should not affect program funding.  
13 
While Agile may reprioritize the order in which items are 
14 
handled, all required project elements must still be 
15 
completed.  For example, in DCX, we reprioritized a 
16 
Financial Assistance tool to help customers better manage 
17 
their energy costs but still implemented a feature to 
18 
provide a Spanish version of energy savings tips in a 
19 
subsequent release. 
20 
Overview of Artificial Intelligence 
21 
Q. 
What is meant by Artificial Intelligence (AI)? 
22 


--- Page 20 ---
CASES 25-E-0072, 25-G-0073 
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK, INC. 
INFORMATION TECHNOLOGY PANEL -- REBUTTAL 
 
19 
 
A. 
Artificial Intelligence (AI) refers to machines simulating 
1 
human intelligence, e.g., the machines are programmed to 
2 
think, learn, and perform tasks that would otherwise 
3 
require human cognition, such as problem-solving, language 
4 
understanding, and decision-making.  It is comprised of 
5 
various technologies, including machine learning (ML), 
6 
Generative AI (GenAI) and Agentic AI.   
7 
Q. 
Explain ML, GenAI and Agentic AI. 
8 
A. 
These three categories are types of AI: 
9 
• ML is a subset of artificial intelligence that enables 
10 
systems to learn from data, identify patterns, and make 
11 
decisions or predictions. It uses algorithms to improve 
12 
its performance as it processes more data over time.  Con 
13 
Edison uses ML for identifying possible safety 
14 
conditions, forecasting call center volumes and 
15 
optimizing staffing, and understanding impacts of new 
16 
grid technologies to support the clean energy transition. 
17 
• Generative AI is a type of artificial intelligence that 
18 
creates content, like new text, images, or videos.  
19 
• Agentic AI is a type of AI that can act autonomously and 
20 
make decisions based on its understanding of the 
21 
environment. It performs tasks, solves problems, and 
22 
adapts to new situations without human intervention, 
23 
leveraging machine learning and reasoning capabilities. 
24 
Q. 
What is a large language model (LLM)? 
25 


--- Page 21 ---
CASES 25-E-0072, 25-G-0073 
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK, INC. 
INFORMATION TECHNOLOGY PANEL -- REBUTTAL 
 
20 
 
A. 
LLM is a type of artificial intelligence algorithm designed 
1 
to understand and generate human language.  By analyzing a 
2 
wide variety of data, it learns to predict and create 
3 
coherent, contextually relevant responses, making it useful 
4 
for writing, translation, and conversation.  These LLMs can 
5 
be found and accessed on AI platforms offered by companies 
6 
like Microsoft and Google.

12 
implemented Agentic AI systems.

Capital and O&M Costs in IT Projects 
20 
Q. 
Please explain the interplay between IT capital and O&M 
21 
costs. 
22 


--- Page 24 ---
CASES 25-E-0072, 25-G-0073 
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK, INC. 
INFORMATION TECHNOLOGY PANEL -- REBUTTAL 
 
23 
 
A. 
For IT projects and programs, capital and O&M costs are 
1 
tightly tied together.  Costs during design, build, and 
2 
deployment phases use capital funding.  After a brief 
3 
stabilization period, the costs to operate and maintain the 
4 
system are required to use O&M funding.  Once the system or 
5 
application is deployed, it immediately incurs costs to be 
6 
reliably operated and maintained.  Examples of these costs 
7 
include vendor licensing fees, vendor support, and 
8 
resources to monitor the system, remediate operational 
9 
issues, and apply patches.  
10 
Q. 
Is this need for immediate maintenance and support costs 
11 
typical for traditional utility projects? 
12 
A. 
No. IT projects do not follow the traditional utility 
13 
infrastructure model.  Traditionally, once gas, electric, 
14 
or steam utility infrastructure is built, its maintenance 
15 
needs are somewhat limited for the initial 5-10 years, as 
16 
the assets are brand new and have yet to be affected by 
17 
wear and tear.   
18 
IT projects and programs must be maintained, upgraded and 
19 
patched, and sometimes require new coding to meet users’ 
20 
needs, from the first day they are deployed. It’s akin to 
21 
putting a pool in the backyard.  From day one, the pool 
22 
must be regularly maintained, cleaned, and new equipment 
23 


--- Page 25 ---
CASES 25-E-0072, 25-G-0073 
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK, INC. 
INFORMATION TECHNOLOGY PANEL -- REBUTTAL 
 
24 
 
purchased for the pool to maintain its utility.  And once a 
1 
crack emerges, it must be addressed immediately, or the 
2 
condition will grow to require a more extensive and 
3 
expensive solution.  
4

Q. 
Why? 
1 
A. 
Capital and O&M funding is generally aimed at two different 
2 
goals.  Capital is used to either replace a degraded asset 
3 
or to create a new one.  O&M addresses the daily cost of 
4 
operating and maintaining existing assets.  The “O&M tail” 
5 
accompanying most projects increases O&M costs as capital 
6 
spending increases.  But that is the only instance of this 
7 
type of relationship, and the timing of the funding needs 
8 
are different.  
9 
Capital and O&M costs can be impacted by different cost 
10 
drivers. For example, capital is driven by software 
11 
development, project management, data migration, testing, 
12 
and contingency costs. In comparison, O&M costs can be 
13 
driven by the frequency of needed updates, user base size, 
14 
technology advancements, and Service Level Agreements.  
15 
For example, Staff recommends decreases to the 
16 
Telecommunications O&M Program, citing reductions to what 
17 
they believe are associated capital programs and would 
18 
theoretically reduce the Company’s need for the level of 
19 
O&M funding requested.  However, the incremental increase 
20 
in telecommunications operating and maintenance expenses is 
21 
primarily driven by expected tariff increases imposed by 
22 
public carriers.  This instance and others like it are 
23 
discussed in more detail later in our testimony.  
24

demands of forward-looking projects, particularly those 
1 
driven by new or evolving compliance obligations or 
2 
technological shifts. Historical averages do not reflect 
3 
future cost structures or innovation needs. Using 
4 
historical averages as a benchmark anchors budgets to 
5 
legacy baselines and assumes that past spending is a 
6 
reliable predictor of future needs, which is often not the 
7 
case in IT.  
8 
Q. 
Does this method account for projects with no historical 
9 
spend or those in early development stages? 
10 
A. 
No. Some projects lack historical spending data, rendering 
11 
this method inapplicable or misleading. Applying it 
12 
uniformly risks arbitrary or unjustified reductions. We 
13 
leverage our existing platforms, experience, benchmarking 
14 
with other utilities, and consultants to build estimates 
15 
for these projects.  
16 
Q. 
Does this method risk reinforcing outdated spending 
17 
patterns rather than encouraging strategic transformation? 
18 
A. 
Yes. By using historical trends, this method may entrench 
19 
legacy systems and delay necessary modernization. It could 
20 
also negatively impact departments that have historically 
21 
underspent due to resource constraints or deferred 
22 
investments. 
23 


--- Page 38 ---
CASES 25-E-0072, 25-G-0073 
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK, INC. 
INFORMATION TECHNOLOGY PANEL -- REBUTTAL 
 
37 
 
Q. 
Why is reduction of an IT project budget to the amount 
1 
indicated by a linear forecast an unreasonable adjustment 
2 
methodology? 
3 
A. 
IT investments often follow a “step-function” pattern—
4 
large, upfront capital outlays for system overhauls, 
5 
followed by periods of lower maintenance spending. Linear 
6 
forecasting assumes smooth, incremental growth, which is 
7 
rarely the case in IT. Projects often involve step-function 
8 
investments (e.g., platform migrations) or cyclical 
9 
upgrades, making linear extrapolation an 
10 
oversimplification. Further, each IT project has a unique 
11 
lifecycle: planning, development, testing, deployment, and 
12 
maintenance. Linear forecasting flattens these phases into 
13 
a trend line, ignoring the fact that costs are not evenly 
14 
distributed across time. In addition, Staff states that the 
15 
trending method “incorporates actual real-world market 
16 
conditions and the Company's current practices and spending 
17 
patterns.” (pg. 13). However, it fails to note that these 
18 
are historic “real world conditions” that are changing and 
19 
will continue to do to.  As virtually every mutual fund 
20 
prospectus tells us “Past success does not guarantee future 
21 
performance,” yet Staff would seem to assert that it does. 
22 


--- Page 39 ---
CASES 25-E-0072, 25-G-0073 
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK, INC. 
INFORMATION TECHNOLOGY PANEL -- REBUTTAL 
 
38 
 
Q. 
Why is adjusting certain IT project requests to 70 percent 
1 
of their proposed value an unreasonable adjustment 
2 
methodology? 
3 
A. 
Applying a blanket 70 percent funding level assumes 
4 
homogeneity in project maturity, risk, and necessity, which 
5 
undermines the principle of individualized project 
6 
evaluation. This approach risks underfunding critical 
7 
infrastructure while overcorrecting for perceived 
8 
inefficiencies in others. 
9 
Q. 
Is this method consistent with Agile methodologies? 
10 
A. 
No. Modern IT projects often use Agile methodologies, 
11 
where, as previously discussed, scope and priorities evolve 
12 
based on feedback and iterative development. Linear 
13 
forecasting assumes fixed trajectories, which contradicts 
14 
Agile’s adaptive nature. 
15

3

4

5

6

7

8

9

10 
already provide meaningful insight into expected costs. 
11

12 
in structured planning processes and historical 
13 
analogs. While the Company bucketed the scoping percentages 
14 
as spelled out in our response to DPS-25-596, these 
15 
percentages reflected the overall status of every activity 
16 
that must be completed before a project is 100% scoped.  
17 
But the preliminary phases of the process provides more 
18 
intelligence to the final expected costs than Staff appears 
19 
to give it credit for.

15

16 
rather than documented misuse or overestimation.

refinement, which is especially important in dynamic IT

INFORMATION TECHNOLOGY PANEL -- REBUTTAL 
 
54 
 
Q. 
Please respond to this recommendation. 
1 
A. 
From an IT governance and operational standpoint, this 
2 
approach introduces more complexity than value. IT projects 
3 
cannot remain in an as-is state without increased cyber 
4 
risks, reduced performance and support, and, eventually, 
5 
becoming obsolete. The Company must align applications with 
6 
vendor roadmaps. Staff’s recommendation to parse all IT 
7 
projects into “maintenance” versus “enhancement” 
8 
components, while well-intentioned, is impractical, 
9 
inefficient, and misaligned with how modern IT systems are 
10 
developed and managed.  
11 
Q. 
Please explain. 
12 
A. 
Most IT initiatives are inherently integrated. A single 
13 
system upgrade may simultaneously replace aging 
14 
infrastructure (maintenance), improve performance 
15 
(enhancement), address security vulnerabilities 
16 
(compliance), and enable new features (strategic). 
17 
Attempting to isolate these elements into discrete cost 
18 
categories would be artificial and misleading. It would 
19 
also risk misrepresenting the true scope and value of the 
20 
investment. Furthermore, the line between “maintaining 
21 
current systems” and “adding new functionality” is often 
22 


--- Page 56 ---
CASES 25-E-0072, 25-G-0073 
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK, INC. 
INFORMATION TECHNOLOGY PANEL -- REBUTTAL 
 
55 
 
blurred. Is replacing an aging server with a faster model 
1 
maintenance or enhancement? Is improving a user interface 
2 
to reduce errors a performance upgrade or a reliability 
3 
fix? These distinctions are not only subjective but also 
4 
vary across teams and projects, potentially leading to 
5 
inconsistent reporting and reduced comparability. 
6

Internal PSRs are currently prepared consistent with 
14

16 
receives and meets with the Company to review the Quarterly 
17 
Capital Expenditure Report. During the meeting Staff can 
18 
request information on the status of any project in the IT 
19 
portfolio.

Q. 
Staff recommends that the Company parse total costs by 
1 
feasibility, design, development, and 
2 
production/implementation for all IT White Papers in future 
3

4

5 
A. 
While cost transparency is essential, the proposed parsing 
6 
requirement is not the right mechanism. It imposes a rigid 
7 
structure on a dynamic process, increases administrative 
8 
burden, and risks distorting the true nature of IT 
9 
investments. A more balanced approach would be to enhance 
10 
narrative justifications and cost-benefit clarity within 
11 
existing documentation frameworks. Forcing a Waterfall-
12 
style cost breakdown misrepresents how IT work is actually 
13 
performed, may lead to inaccurate or arbitrary allocations, 
14 
and is unlikely to yield meaningful insight.

22 


--- Page 80 ---
CASES 25-E-0072, 25-G-0073 
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK, INC.

79 
 
schedule is based on when the vendor releases the product, 
1 
not when it is implemented. When the Company implemented 
2 
CC&B in 2023, it implemented version 2.8, which Oracle 
3 
released in 2021. Accordingly, Oracle will end support for 
4 
this version of CC&B in May 2029.   
5 
Because of its implementation schedule, the Company could 
6

7

8 
version 2.9, in 2022, while the Company’s CC&B 
9 
implementation was already underway. The Company evaluated 
10 
version 2.9 and determined that it did not provide enough 
11 
incremental value to warrant the additional cost and work 
12 
needed to change the go-live version midstream and the 
13 
additional risk that doing so would have posed to the 
14 
project’s timeline and the success of implementation. This 
15 
decision was consistent with a generally accepted principle 
16 
of large-scale IT implementations known as “N-1.” This 
17 
refers to a strategic approach of choosing one version 
18 
previous to the most recent version to balance stability 
19 
and innovation by going live with a more proven solution, 
20 
thereby reducing risk of unforeseen or unknown issues that 
21 
might arise with a newer, less thoroughly vetted version.  
22

Q. 
How does the Company respond to Staff’s argument that the 
1 
Company has not provided evidence of customer 
2 
expectations?   
3 
A. 
The Company disagrees. If the Company does not continue to 
4 
invest in its digital platforms, their value and use will 
5 
erode. As with any technology in today’s increasingly 
6 
digital world, only maintaining the status quo is 
7 
insufficient to meet rising customer expectations. The 
8 
business considerations of these rising expectations are 
9 
well summarized by the saying “The last best experience 
10 
that anyone has anywhere becomes the minimum expectation 
11 
for the experience they want everywhere,” coined by Bridget 
12 
Van Kralingen, former VP of IBM Global Markets.   
13 
For the Company, this means our customers are not comparing 
14 
our customer service or digital experiences to their water 
15 
utilities or tax payment digital experiences; they are 
16 
comparing them to airline, banking, and retail experiences 
17 
that are delivering efficient and personalized digital 
18 
experiences across multiple interaction channels. If Con 
19 
Edison’s digital experience is eroded due to a lack of 
20 
investment, the Company’s digitally inclined residential,

A.

1

9 
a customers’ needs. The CEP will improve business 
10

11

13

14

and complexity.

1

2

3

4

5

6 
require access controls to those systems.

12 
may not apply in future periods. In the absence of a CEP, 
13

incomplete views of customer interactions, which 
15 
complicates the efforts of CSRs to assist customers. The 
16 
current lack of systems integration limits employees’ 
17 
ability to effectively promote integrated solutions that 
18

Q. 
Please comment on the downward-only reconciliation for 
1 
CARE’s O&M budget.  
2 
A. 
A downward-only reconciliation of O&M for CARE is 
3 
inappropriate. First, Staff has not provided any evidence 
4 
that there is a need for this reconciliation.   
5 
Moreover, a downward reconciliation exposes the Company to 
6 
all downside risk of cost overruns, while providing no 
7 
opportunity for recovery if costs exceed the forecast. 
8 
Imposing a downward-only reconciliation would hinder the 
9 
Company’s ability to leverage an Agile and iterative 
10 
approach to product development and enhancement, and to 
11

Billing and Payment Enhancements 
1 
Q. 
Please explain the Billing and Payment Enhancements 
2 
program.  
3 
A. 
As discussed in Exhibit__(IT-5), the Billing and Payment 
4 
Enhancements Program will maintain necessary processes and 
5 
introduce new technology upgrades to improve the customer 
6 
billing and payment experience. These enhancements will 
7 
empower customers by increasing billing clarity, payment 
8 
offerings, and program awareness. Specific activities 
9 
outlined in this proposal include funding to maintain 
10 
existing technology systems and processes, installation of 
11 
modern kiosks in customer walk-in centers and non-Company 
12 
locations, expansion of kiosk payment options to debit and 
13 
credit card payment and digital wallets (i.e., Apple Pay, 
14 
Google Pay, PayPal, and Venmo), and launching personalized 
15 
video messaging to target customers for enrollment in the 
16 
Company’s paperless billing, budget billing, and auto-pay 
17 
programs.    
18 
Q. 
Why is it necessary for the Company to replace its current 
19 
kiosks with modern, upgraded kiosks? 
20 
A. 
Payments are one of the primary reasons customers visit the 
21 
Company’s walk-in centers. To better meet these customers’ 
22

1 
Q.

2 
request. 
3 
A. 
By leveraging industry-leading software and systems, the 
4

5

6 
Company issue timely and accurate bills to customers and 
7 
avoid costs associated with manual work, such as those 
8 
stemming from estimated and delayed bills and rate 
9 
validation.   
10

11

12 
Automation (RPA), (2) improving workflow and exception 
13 
handling with PEGA Customer Experience System software 
14 
(PEGA CXS), and (3) enhancing the Knowledge Advance (KA) 
15 
platform for employees. This proposal, however, 
16 
predominantly seeks funding for two essential system 
17 
upgrades:  
18 
• 
The migration of RPA operations to a new enterprise 
19 
platform (PowerAutomate), which is necessary to replace 
20 
legacy manual processes and realize cost avoidance by 
21 
automating high-volume, rules-based tasks. While the 
22 
enterprise project related to the migration to the Power 
23 
Automate platform is discussed in Exhibit__(IT-9) Power 
24

6

The retrofit and expansion of the PEGA CXS case

management system, which is critical for modernizing

Program are fundamentally different than the Company’s day-

15

17

18 
the event of a breach (please see 
19 
https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/SpecialPublications/NIST.

AMI Systems Sustainability  
7 
Q. 
Please explain the AMI Systems Sustainability program. 
8 
A. 
The Company’s AMI Systems Sustainability program is 
9 
comprised of two overall requirements: maintaining the 
10 
systems and implementing enhancements. 
11 
To stay compliant with vendors’ maintenance roadmap 
12 
requirements as well as to stay cybersecure, the Company’s 
13 
AMI systems must be upgraded regularly.  Maintaining the 
14 
system requires periodic upgrades as required to hardware 
15 
and software as well as hardware replacements every five 
16 
years.  Vendor support is contingent on meeting vendor 
17 
recommended maintenance and refresh schedules.     
18 
Additionally, IT regularly receives requests from users and 
19 
regulators that require the AMI systems to be enhanced or 
20 
changed. 
21 


--- Page 234 ---
CASES 25-E-0072, 25-G-0073

233 
 
This program aims to do the work to meet the below 
1 
requirements based on above-mentioned Upgrades and 
2 
Enhancements needs.    
3 
• Head End System (HES): Annual upgrades to enhance 
4 
functionality, security, and vendor support.  
5 
• Meter Asset Management System (MAMS): Centralized data 
6 
repository, version control, automated deployment, and 
7 
streamlined workflows.  
8 
• Meter Data Management System (MDMS): Upgrade to latest 
9 
version, implement PSC-mandated bill rates, and improve 
10 
integration and efficiency.  
11 
• Hardware Upgrades: Refresh servers and storage, implement 
12 
high availability, and adopt containerization (e.g., 
13 
Kubernetes) for scalability.  
14 
• Profield Encore (PFE): Improve inventory control, 
15 
documentation, and large meter installations.  
16 
• Custom Applications: Enhance data sync, real-time alerts, 
17 
alarm mapping, and billing accuracy.

hinder the ability to support new technologies and

2 
Q. 
Is reducing the scope of this project an effective 
3 
strategy? 
4 
A. 
No. Breaking up the work into smaller, disjointed projects 
5 
can impact project synergies and increase overall costs in 
6 
the long term. For example, there are synergies in 
7 
executing remediation projects together. For example, two 
8 
data centers at the same location require similar upgrades 
9 
to their power and cooling systems. Upgrading the two data 
10 
centers at the same location simultaneously can leverage 
11 
project synergies, reducing overall costs and improving 
12 
efficiency. Fragmenting these projects would forfeit these 
13 
synergies, leading to higher individual project costs and 
14 
inefficiencies. Additionally, spreading out the work can 
15 
result in repeated mobilization costs, prolonged project

Enterprise Architecture Modernization 
4 
Q. 
Please explain Enterprise Architecture Modernization. 
5 
A. 
The Enterprise Architecture Modernization project is a two-
6 
prong project intended to provide both IT and users with 
7 
information, including current technology status, as well 
8 
as to help IT and users understand architecture 
9 
requirements when implementing projects. Currently, if a 
10 
user group suggests implementing a technology, Enterprise 
11 
Architecture (the Company organization responsible for 
12 
determining how projects are architected and designed) 
13 
manually reviews each suggested technology and advise users 
14 
on acceptability or obsolescence. The Company employs 
15 
almost 800 products, and Enterprise Architecture reviews 
16 
80-100 suggested products annually. This project will 
17 
automate the review and provide users with this 
18 
information, including providing proactive blueprints and 
19 
frameworks for IT and users to understand technology and 
20 
obsolescence of tools.   
21 


--- Page 337 ---
CASES 25-E-0072, 25-G-0073 
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK, INC. 
INFORMATION TECHNOLOGY PANEL -- REBUTTAL 
 
336 
 
These products will continue the Company’s progress toward 
1 
an enterprise technology view as well as using consistent 
2 
technology when developing and implementing new use cases.   
3 
Q. 
What are the key benefits of proceeding with the full 
4 
project scope? 
5 
A. 
Implementing a standardized architecture framework and 
6 
complimenting governance processes provides secure, 
7 
efficient, and consistent technology adoption. It will help 
8 
the Company better understand the existing technology debt 
9 
and make it available to IT and users, so there is a 
10 
common, overall understanding of the technology before its 
11 
even suggested.  These improvements directly support cyber 
12 
resilience, operational excellence, and compliance with 
13 
industry standards.

Q. 
And finally, what about the strategic misalignments that 
5 
the City refers to? 
6 
A. 
This appears to mostly be driven by the dynamic tension 
7 
that exists between on-premises and cloud solutions, and 
8 
the work needed to safely transition from one to the other, 
9 
and the City speaks to this issue specifically as it 
10 
relates to our Mainframe Exit program. Although cloud is a 
11 
planned initiative, migrations can take years to complete. 
12 
As such, on-premises data centers and applications need to 
13 
be supported until applications are re-platformed or 
14 
retired and this will take time to achieve. In addition, a 
15 
limited number of applications, such as SCADA, will remain 
16 
on premise for the foreseeable future and these must also 
17 
be properly maintained.
